witnessing that (a, b) is a risky pair are in shadj(b0) and in turn “trap” b in
shadj(b0).
Proposition 39. Let (a, b) ∈I be a risky pair, let b0 ∈B with a ∥b0 in P, and suppose
that for some nonnegative integer j, shadj(b0) and shadj(b) have the same initial element. If
a ∈shadj(b0), then b ∈shadj(b0).
Proof. See Figure 15. Since (a, b) is a risky pair, there exist b′, b′′ ∈B as in (r1) and (r2). By
Observation 22, we obtain that b′ and b′′ lie in the interior of shadj(b0).
Let x and y be the initial and terminal elements of shadj(b0), respectively. By Proposition 30
applied to b′, either WL(y) is left of WL(b′) (as (b′, y) is an inside pair) or y < b′ in P. In
the case where y < b′ in P, by Proposition 28, WL(y) is a subpath of WL(b′). Similarly, by
Proposition 30 applied to b′′, either WR(b′′) is left of WR(y) or WR(y) is a subpath of WR(b′′).
We claim that
no witnessing path from x0 to b in P is left of WL(y), and
no witnessing path from x0 to b in P is right of WR(y).
(4)
Indeed, if U is a witnessing path from x0 to b in P and U is left of WL(y), then U is left of
WL(b′). However, this implies that WL(b) is left of WL(b′), a contradiction. The proof of the
statement for rightmost paths goes analogously.
First, suppose that neither of WL(b), WL(y) is a subpath of the other and that neither of
WR(b), WR(y) is a subpath of the other.
By (4), it follows that WL(y) is left of WL(b)
and WR(b) is left of WR(y). Therefore, (b, y) is an inside pair, and by Proposition 31, b ∈
shadj(y) = shadj(b0).
If WL(b) is a subpath of WL(y), then b ∈shadj(b0). Similarly, if WR(b) is a subpath of WR(y),
then b ∈shadj(b0).
Finally, it remains to consider the case, where WL(y) is a subpath of WL(b) or WR(y) is a
subpath of WR(b). Assume that WL(y) is a subpath of WL(b), the proof in the other case is
symmetric. It follows that y ⩽b in P. Recall that either WL(y) is left of WL(b′) or WL(y) is
a subpath of WL(b′). If WL(y) is left of WL(b′), then WL(b) is left of WL(b′), which is false as

b′ witnesses (r1) for (a, b). Hence, WL(y) is a subpath of WL(b′). Since b′ is in the interior of

5.2. Escape number and address. Let (a, b) ∈I. We define the escape number of (a, b) to
be the least nonnegative integer j such that a /∈shadj(b). Note that this is well-defined as if
j = sd(b) + 1, then shadj(b) = ∅. For each θ ∈{0, 1}, we let
Iθ = {(a, b) ∈I : the escape number of (a, b) is j with j ≡θ mod 2}.
Note that I is partitioned into I0 and I1.
We define the address of (a, b) to be the pair (j, x), where j is the escape number of (a, b) and
x is the initial element of shadj(b). See Figure 16.
We prove now a comprehensive lemma that provides structural information about strict al-
ternating cycles with all pairs being risky and in Iθ for a fixed θ ∈{0, 1}. See Figure 17.
Lemma 40. Let θ ∈{0, 1}, and let ((a1, b1), . . . , (ak, bk)) be a strict alternating cycle in P with
all the pairs being risky pairs in Iθ. Then there is a nonnegative integer j and an element x
of P such that for every α ∈[k], the address of (aα, bα) is (j, x); and for all distinct α, β ∈[k],
bβ /∈shadj(bα).
Proof. The indices in [k] are considered cyclically, e.g. ak+1 = a1. For each α ∈[k], let (jα, xα)
be the address of (aα, bα). Set j = min{jα : α ∈[k]}. Fix γ ∈[k] such that j = jγ and let
x = xγ. Eventually, we will show that (j, x) is the common address of (aα, bα) for all α ∈[k].
The proof is split into three intermediate statements encapsulated by the following claims.
Claim 41. For all α ∈[k], if j > 0, then x is the terminal element of shadj−1(bα).
Proof. If j = 0, then the claim holds vacuously, thus, suppose that j > 0 and let α ∈[k].
Since j is at most the escape number of (aα, bα), the element aα lies in shadj−1(bα). Let Bα
be the block of shadj−1(bα) such that aα lies in Bα. Since aα ⩽bα+1 and aα ∥bα in P, by

Lemma 40 shows that every strict alternating cycle with all the pairs in Iθ for
some θ ∈{0, 1} “looks like this”. Note, however, that the shadow depth of each

in the cycle (as it indeed is for b1).

shadj−1(bα+1) ⊆shadj−1(max Bα) ⊆shadj−1(bα).
Since these inclusions hold for all α ∈[k] cyclically, we conclude that for all α ∈[k],
shadj−1(bα+1) = shadj−1(max Bα) = shadj−1(bα).
Since shadj−1(bα) are the same for all α ∈[k], their terminal blocks also coincide. Let B be
the common terminal block. Recall that for every α ∈[k], bα+1 lies in the terminal block
of shadj−1(bα+1), thus, B = Bα. Since x = xγ is the terminal element of shadj−1(bγ), we
conclude that x = max B and the assertion holds.

Claim 42. Let α, δ ∈[k] with δ ̸= α −1 and such that aδ lies in the interior of shadj(bα).
Then, aα lies in the interior of shadj(bα).
Proof. Without loss of generality, assume that α = 1. Note that δ ̸= k. We show that if
β ∈[k −1] and aβ lies in the interior of shadj(b1), then either β = 1 or aβ−1 is also in the
interior of shadj(b1). The statement of the claim immediately follows.
Suppose that β ∈{2, . . . , k−1} and aβ lies in the interior of shadj(b1). Recall that by Claim 41,
shadj(b1) and shadj(bβ) have a common initial element (namely x). Since the pair (aβ, bβ) is
a risky pair, aβ ∥b1 in P, and aβ is in the interior of shadj(b1), by Proposition 39, bβ lies in
the interior of shadj(b1). Since aβ−1 ⩽bβ in P and 1 ̸= β, by Observation 22, we obtain that
aβ−1 is in the interior of shadj(b1), as desired.
◁

Claim 43. For all α ∈[k], we have jα = j.
Proof. Suppose to the contrary that there is some α ∈[k] with jα ̸= j. Since we consider
only pairs in Iθ and j is the minimum value of the escape number among all the pairs in the
strict alternating cycle, we have jα ⩾j + 2. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
jα+1 = j.
The argument is illustrated in Figure 18. By definition of the escape number, the element aα
lies in the interior of shadj+1(bα). Since aα ⩽bα+1 in P, by Observation 22, bα+1 lies in the
interior of shadj+1(bα). By Proposition 29.(iv), we have shadj(bα) = shadj(bα+1).
Let y be the terminal element of shadj(bα) = shadj(bα+1). It follows that y ⩽bα and y ⩽bα+1
in P, hence, by definition of strict alternating cycles, for every β ∈[k], we have aβ ∥y in P.
In particular, aα−1 ∥y in P. Since aα−1 ⩽bα in P, by Observation 22, the element aα−1 lies
in the interior of shadj(bα) = shadj(bα+1). Therefore, by Claim 42, aα+1 lies in the interior
of shadj(bα+1), which contradicts jα+1 = j and ends the proof of the claim.
◁
To complete the proof of the lemma, it suffices to prove that for all distinct α, β ∈[k], we
have bβ /∈shadj(bα). Suppose to the contrary that there are distinct α, β ∈[k] such that
bβ ∈shadj(bα).
Since bα ∥bβ in P, we obtain a stronger statement that bβ lies in the
interior of shadj(bα). Since α ̸= β, we have aβ−1 ∥bα in P. This combined with the fact
that aβ−1 ⩽bβ in P and Observation 21 implies that aβ−1 lies in the interior of shadj(bα).
Therefore, by Claim 42, we obtain that aα lies in the interior of shadj(bα), which yields jα > j,
which contradicts Claim 43. This completes the proof of the lemma.
□
5.3. Dangerous pairs. In this subsection, we refine the notion of risky pairs to the so-called
dangerous pairs. We say that (a, b) ∈I is a dangerous pair if


## Page 41

41
(d1) there exists b′ ∈B with a ⩽b′ in P such that b′ is left of b, and
(d2) there exists b′′ ∈B with a ⩽b′′ in P such that b is left of b′′.
See examples of dangerous and non-dangerous pairs in Figure 14. Dangerous pairs are the
only “interesting” pairs in the following sense. Later on, we will apply Lemma 40, to reduce
the problem only to pairs so that in every strict alternating cycle, the elements b are ordered
left-to-right in the sense introduced in Subsection 4.4. We show that, assuming this property,
the non-dangerous pairs have low dimension.
Proposition 44. Let I′ ⊆I be a set of pairs that are not dangerous such that for every strict
alternating cycle ((a1, b1), . . . , (ak, bk)) in P contained in I′, for all α, β ∈[k] with α ̸= β,
either bα left of bβ or bα right of bβ. Then, dimP (I′) ⩽2.
Proof. Let I′
1 be all the pairs in I′ that do not satisfy (d1) and let I′
2 be all the pairs in I′ that
do not satisfy (d2). Note that I′ = I′
1 ∪I′
2. We claim that I′
1 and I′
2 are reversible. We will
prove that I′
1 is reversible, the proof for I′
2 is symmetric. Suppose to the contrary that I′
1 is not
reversible, and so by Proposition 3, I′
1 contains a strict alternating cycle ((a1, b1), . . . , (ak, bk)).
Let α ∈[k]. By the assumption, either bα is left of bα+1 or bα+1 is left of bα (we consider the
indices cyclically, e.g. bk+1 = b1). Since (aα, bα) does not satisfy (d1), aα ⩽bα+1 in P we have
that bα+1 is right of bα. However, this cannot hold cyclically for all α ∈[k]. The contradiction
completes the proof.
□
Proposition 45. Let (a, b) ∈I be a dangerous pair and let d ∈B such that either d is left of
b or b is left of d. Then a /∈shad(d).
Proof. Assume that d is left of b. When b is left of d, the proof is symmetric. Let b′′ ∈B
witness (d2) for (a, b). Since b′′ /∈shad(b) and a ⩽b′′ in P, by Observation 22, we obtain
a /∈shad(b).
Suppose to the contrary that a ∈shad(d). Since a ⩽b′′ in P, there is a witnessing path
W from a to b′′ in P. Let u be the first element in B on W. It follows that u ∈shad(d).
Moreover, since a /∈shad(b) and a ⩽u in P, by Observation 22, we obtain u /∈shad(b). Since
d left of b, by Proposition 36.(L), u is left of b. Since u is left of b and b is left of b′′, u is
left of b′′ (by Proposition 37). In particular, u /∈shad(b′′), however, u ⩽b′′ in P, which is a
contradiction with Proposition 33, hence, indeed, a /∈shad(d).
□
Corollary 46. Let (a, b) ∈I be a dangerous pair, let (d1) be witnessed by b′ ∈B, and let (d2)
be witnessed by b′′ ∈B. Then
a /∈shad(b′),
a /∈shad(b′′),
and a /∈B.
Proof. The first two items are direct corollaries of Proposition 45. For the last item, if a ∈B,
then since a ⩽b′ in P, by Proposition 33, shad(a) ⊆shad(b′), which contradicts a /∈shad(b′),
and proves that a /∈B.
□

we introduce the following notion. We say that an instance (P, x0, G, e−∞, I) is a good instance
if
(I6) a /∈shad(b) for every (a, b) ∈I;
(I7) for every strict alternating cycle ((a1, b1), . . . , (ak, bk)) in P contained in I, for all
distinct α, β ∈[k], either bα is left of bβ or bα is right of bβ;

For a given set X and I ⊆X × X, we define
π1(I) = {a ∈X : there exists b′ ∈X with (a, b′) ∈I} and
π2(I) = {b ∈X : there exists a′ ∈X with (a′, b) ∈I}.
We say that a good instance (P, x0, G, e−∞, I) is maximal if
(I9) I is the set of all pairs (a, b) ∈Inc(P) such that a ∈π1(I), b ∈π2(I), a ̸∈shad(b), and
(a, b) is dangerous.
Note that it is not immediate if a good instance is “contained” in some maximal good instance.
This issue is resolved in the next proposition.
Proposition 48. For every good instance (P, x0, G, e−∞, I), there exists I+ ⊆Inc(P) such
that I ⊆I+ ⊆Inc(P) ∩(π1(I) × π2(I)) and (P, x0, G, e−∞, I+) is a maximal good instance.
Proof. Let I = (P, x0, G, e−∞, I) be a good instance and let I+ be the set of all pairs (a, b) ∈
Inc(P) such that there exist elements a′, b′ in P with (a′, b), (a, b′) ∈I, a ̸∈shad(b), and (a, b)
is dangerous. Since I satisfies (I6) and (I8), we have I ⊆I+. Let I+ = (P, x0, G, e−∞, I+).
It is clear that I+ satisfies (I1)–(I6) and (I8). By definition, I+ also satisfies (I9). Thus, it
suffices to argue that I+ satisfies (I7). Let ((a1, b1), . . . , (ak, bk)) be a strict alternating cycle
in P contained in I+. We plan to apply Lemma 40. To this end, note that since a /∈shad(b)
for every (a, b) ∈I+, the escape number of each pair (a, b) ∈I+ is 0. Therefore, by Lemma 40
applied in the context of I+, for all distinct α, β ∈[k], either bα is left of bβ or bα is right of
bβ. This shows that I+ satisfies (I7), and thus, ends the proof.
□
6. Topology of a maximal good instance
The tools built so far give ground for the first two steps of the proof of Theorem 1. The unfold-
ing technique presented in the preliminaries (see Lemma 12) allows to reduce the statement
of Theorem 1 to a statement on dimension of an instance (see Corollary 13). In Section 5, we
saw how to further reduce the problem to a statement on a good instance (see Corollary 47)
and a maximal good instance (see Proposition 48). It remains to prove the following bound
on dimension in maximal good instances.
Theorem 49. For every maximal good instance (P, x0, G, e−∞, I), we have
dimP (I) ⩽16 seP (I)6 · (seP (I) + 3)2.
Before we prove Theorem 49, we need some more theory. In this section, we work with a fixed
maximal good instance (P, x0, G, e−∞, I).
6.1. Exposed paths. Let
B = {b in P : x0 ⩽b in P} and
A = {a in P : there exists (a, b) ∈I}.
Since every (a, b) ∈I is dangerous (see (I8)), by Corollary 46, A ∩B = ∅. Let a ∈A and
b ∈B with a < b in P. We say that a witnessing path W from a to b in P is exposed if b is
the only element of W in B. For every a ∈A, we define
Y (a) = {y ∈B : a < y in P and a /∈shad(y)} and
Z(a) = {z ∈Y (a) : there exists an exposed witnessing path from a to z in P}.

Observe that every element in Y (a) generates at least one element in Z(a).
Indeed, let
y ∈Y (a) and let W be a witnessing path from a to y in P. Let z be the first element in B
in W. Since z ⩽y in P, we have shad(z) ⊆shad(y) (by Proposition 33), and so, z ∈Y (a).
Moreover, a[W]z is an exposed witnessing path from a to z in P, and so, z ∈Z(a). Note that
when z ∈Z(a), there can be a witnessing path from a to z that is not exposed. In particular,
the set Z(a) is not necessarily an antichain. See Figure 19.
Before, we proceed with the material on exposed paths and elements in Z(a), we state a
technical corollary of Corollary 46. It follows since (by (I8)) all the pairs in I are dangerous.
We will use it extensively.
Corollary 50. Let (a, b) ∈I and let d ∈B such that either b is left of d or d is left of b.
Then, a /∈shad(d). Moreover, if a < d in P, then d ∈Y (a).
Proposition 51. Let a ∈A, b ∈B, y ∈Y (a), and z, z′ ∈Z(a). Then,
(i) if a /∈shad(b), then z /∈int shad(b);
(ii) if z ⩽y in P, then z ∈∂shad(y);
(iii) sd(z) = 0;
(iv) if z ∥z′ in P, then either z is left of z′ or z is right of z′.
Proof. In order to prove (i), suppose that a /∈shad(b) and z ∈shad(b). We argue that z must
be in the boundary of shad(b). Since z ∈Z(a), there is an exposed witnessing path W from
a to z in P. Note that W intersects ∂shad(b). The only element of W in B is z, and all the
elements of ∂shad(b) are in B, hence, z ∈∂shad(b). This completes the proof of (i).
In order to prove (ii) suppose that z ⩽y in P. By Proposition 33, we have shad(z) ⊆shad(y).
In particular, z ∈shad(y).
By (i), z lies on the boundary of shad(y), which proves (ii).
Item (iii) follows from (ii) and Proposition 29.(iii). Finally, suppose that z ∥z′ in P. Since
z′ ∈Z(a) ⊆Y (a), a /∈shad(z). If z′ ∈shad(z), then z′ ∈int shad(z), which is a contradiction

with (i). It follows that z′ /∈shad(z), and symmetrically, we obtain z /∈shad(z′). Therefore,
by Corollary 34, either z is left of z′ or z is right of z′, which proves (iv).
□
Proposition 52. Let a ∈A, b ∈B with a ∥b in P and a /∈shad(b). Let y, y′ ∈Y (a) with
y′ ⩽y in P.
(L) If y is left of b, then y′ is left of b.
(R) If y is right of b, then y′ is right of b.
Proof. We prove only (L) as the proof of (R) is symmetric. Assume that y is left of b. Since
a /∈shad(b), by Observation 22, we have y′ /∈shad(b). Furthermore, since y′ ⩽y in P, we
have, y′ ∈shad(y) (by Proposition 33). Finally, by Proposition 36.(L), since y is left of b, we
obtain that y′ is left of b.
□
Corollary 53. Let (a, b) ∈I and let y, y′ ∈B with a < y′ ⩽y in P.
(L) If y is left of b, then y′ is left of b.
(R) If y is right of b, then y′ is right of b.
Proof. We prove only (L) as the proof of (R) is symmetric.
Assume that y is left of b.
By (I8), (a, b) is dangerous, and so by Proposition 45, a /∈shad(y). Since y′ ⩽y in P, we
have shad(y′) ⊆shad(y) (by Proposition 33). It follows that a /∈shad(y′). In particular,
y, y′ ∈Y (a). By (I6), a /∈shad(b). Finally, we apply Proposition 52.(L) to obtain that y′ is
left of b.
□
For every a ∈A, let M(a) be the family of all the paths of the form M = x0[U]z[V ]a, where
• z ∈Z(a) (we call the element z the peak of M),
• U is a witnessing path from x0 to z in P,
• V is an exposed witnessing path from a to z in P.
Since every path in M(a) starts in x0 and ends in a, no path in M(a) contains another path
from M(a) as a subpath. Therefore, by Observation 10, the relation of being left linearly
orders M(a). Note also that M(a) is nonempty as there exists b ∈B such that (a, b) ∈I, so
by (I8) and (d1), there exists b′ ∈B with a < b′ in P, thus, b′ ∈Y (a), and therefore, Z(a) is
nonempty. In particular, M(a) has a minimum and a maximum element. Let ML(a) ∈M(a)
be such that ML(a) is left of M for every M ∈M(a) with M ̸= ML(a), and let MR(a) ∈M(a)
be such that MR(a) is right of M for every M ∈M(a) with M ̸= MR(a). Moreover, denote
by zL(a) the peak of ML(a) and by zR(a) the peak of MR(a). See Figure 20.
We finish this subsection with a series of simple and intuitive propositions on properties of
ML(a) and zL(a) (resp. MR(a) and zR(a)) for a ∈A. Proposition 54 says that ML(a) contains
WL(zL(a)). Proposition 55 gives x0-consistency of paths in {ML(a) : a ∈A}. Propositions 56
and 57 describe the position of zL(a) within Z(a) and with respect to b when (a, b) ∈I.

x0[ML(a)]zL(a) = WL(zL(a))
and
x0[MR(a)]zR(a) = WR(zR(a)).

to the contrary that x0[ML(a)]zL(a) ̸= WL(zL(a)).
Note that both paths are witnessing
paths from x0 to zL(a). Thus, WL(zL(a)) is left of x0[ML(a)]zL(a). Consider the path M =

Proposition 55. Let a1, a2 ∈A.
(L) The paths ML(a1) and ML(a2) are x0-consistent.
(R) The paths MR(a1) and MR(a2) are x0-consistent.
Proof. We prove only (L) as the proof of (R) is symmetric. Let zi = zL(ai) and Mi = ML(ai)
for each i ∈[2]. If one of the paths M1, M2 is a subpath of the other, then the assertion
follows. Hence, assume without loss of generality that M1 is left of M2. Let u = gcpe(M1, M2).
Suppose to the contrary that the paths are not x0-consistent. Then, there exists an element
v common to both paths with v not in x0[M1]u = x0[M2]u. Note that by Proposition 54
and Proposition 15.(L), v /∈B. The path M = x0[M1]v[M2]a2 is left of M2. We claim that
M ∈M(a2), which contradicts the definition of M2. The path a1[ML(a1)]v[ML(a2)]a2 does
not have elements in B, hence, a1 /∈shad(z1) implies a2 /∈shad(z1). Since additionally the
path z1[M1]v[M2]a2 is an exposed witnessing path from a2 to z1 in P, we have z1 ∈Z(a2).
Moreover, M = x0[M1]z1[M1]v[M2]a2, and so, M ∈M(a2).
□

(L) Either zL(a) is left of z or zL(a) and z are comparable in P.

M ∈M(a) and z is the peak of M. If ML(a) = M, then zL(a) = z, which contradicts the
assumption that z ∥zL(a) in P. Thus, ML(a) is left of M. Let w = gcpe(ML(a), M). If

Proposition 57. Let (a, b) ∈I. Then zL(a) is left of b, and zR(a) is right of b.

The pair (a, b) is a dangerous pair by (I8). By (d1), there exists b′ ∈B with a < b′ in P such
that b′ is left of b. By Corollary 46, a /∈shad(b′). It follows that b′ ∈Y (a). Let z′ ∈Z(a)
with z′ ⩽b′ in P. By Corollary 53, z′ is left of b. By (I6), a /∈shad(b). Since a < zL(a) in P,
by Observation 22, we have
zL(a) /∈shad(b).
(5)
By Proposition 56.(L), either zL(a) is left of z′ or zL(a) and z′ are comparable in P. If zL(a)

zL(a) and z′ are comparable in P. First, assume that zL(a) ⩽z′ in P. Since (a, b) ∈I,
a ⩽zL(a) ⩽z′ ⩽b′ in P, and b′ is left of b, by Corollary 53.(L), we obtain that zL(a) is left
of b.
Finally, assume that z′ < zL(a) in P. In particular, z′ ∈shad(zL(a)) (by Proposition 33),
however, by Proposition 51.(i), z′ does not lie in the interior of shad(zL(a)), thus, z′ is an
element of WL(zL(a)) ∪WR(zL(a)). Next, we claim that
b /∈shad(zL(a)).
(6)
Suppose to the contrary that b ∈shad(zL(a)). By Proposition 30.(R), WR(b) is not right of
WR(zL(a)). If z′ is an element of WR(zL(a)), then WR(z′) is a subpath of WR(zL(a)), although,
since z′ is left of b, this implies that WR(b) is right of WR(zL(a)), which is a contradiction.
Therefore, z′ is an element of WL(zL(a)) and it is not an element of WR(zL(a)). In other
words, z′ is strictly on the left side of some shadow block B of shad(zL(a)). The path ML(a)
is left of M = x0[WL(z′)]z′[W]a, where W is an exposed witnessing path from a to z′ in
P. By Proposition 54, x0[ML(a)]zL(a) = WL(zL(a)). It follows that x0[ML(a)]z′ = x0[M]z′
and the edges following z′ in ML(a) and M are distinct. Moreover, by (sb3), the first edge
of z′[M]a = W is in the interior of B. Since the path W is exposed, all the vertices of W
except z′ are in the interior of B, and so, in shad(zL(a)). In particular, a ∈shad(zL(a)), which
is a contradiction. This contradiction completes the proof of (6), that is, b /∈shad(zL(a)).
Recall that also zL(a) /∈shad(b) (by (5)). Therefore, by Corollary 34, either zL(a) is left of
b or b is left of zL(a). Recall that z′ is an element of WL(zL(a)) ∪WR(zL(a)). If z′ lies on
WL(zL(a)), then WL(z′) is a subpath of WL(zL(a)), and since z′ is left of b, WL(zL(a))) is left
of WL(b), which implies that zL(a) is left of b. Similarly, if z′ lies on WR(zL(a)), then WR(z′)
is a subpath of WR(zL(a)), and since z′ is left of b, WR(zL(a))) is left of WR(b), which implies
that zL(a) is left of b.
□

Proposition 60. Let (a, u, v, U, V, R, q, m, γL, γR) be a region tuple and let b ∈B with b /∈
shad(q) \ {q}. Then
(i) b ∈∂R if and only if b lies in q[WL(u)]u ∪q[WR(v)]v.

(iii) b /∈R if and only if WL(b) is left of γL or WR(b) is right of γR.

and v[V ]m. Since b ∈B and the two paths U, V are exposed, item (i) follows.

in order to conclude the proof of the proposition, we will show the right-to-left direction of

a ∥b′, and a′ ∥d in P are necessary. The necessity of the first two is depicted
with elements c1 and c2 respectively. For the last, note that if a′ ⩽d in P, and
element from outside the region could send a comparability to d through V .
Corollary 61. Let (a, u, v, U, V, R, q, m, γL, γR) be a region tuple and let b ∈B.
(i) If u is left of b and b is left of v, then, b ∈int R.
(ii) If b is left of u or v is left of b, then b /∈R.
Proof. Since q < u in P, we have shad(q) ⊆shad(u) (by Proposition 33). Assume that u is
left of b. In particular, b /∈shad(u), and so, b /∈shad(q). Since WL(u) is left of WL(b), we
have that γL is left of WL(b). Since b is left of v, we have that WR(b) is left of γR. Therefore,
by Proposition 60.(ii), b ∈int R, which completes the proof of (i).
Similarly as above, if b is left of u, b /∈shad(u), and b /∈shad(q); and if v is left of b,
then b /∈shad(v), and b /∈shad(q). If b is left of u, then WL(b) is left of γL, and b /∈R
by Proposition 60.(iii). Symmetrically, if b is right of v, then WR(b) is right of γR, and b /∈R
by Proposition 60.(iii). This gives (ii).
□
We conclude this section with a technical statement that will be used repetitively in the proof
of Lemma 76 (Coloring Lemma).
Proposition 62. Let (a, b), (a′, b′) ∈I and d ∈B with b left of d and d left of b′.
(L) Assume that a < d, a ∥b′, and a′ ∥d in P. Let z′ ∈Z(a′) with z′ ⩽b in P. Let U and V
be exposed witnessing paths from a′ to z′ and from a′ to zR(a′) in P, respectively. Then,
z′ is left of zR(a′) and
a ∈int R(a′, z′, zR(a′), U, V ).
(R) Assume that a′ < d, a′ ∥b, and a ∥d in P. Let z ∈Z(a) with z ⩽b′ in P. Let U and
V be exposed witnessing paths from a to zL(a) and from a to z in P, respectively. Then,
zL(a) is left of z and
a′ ∈int R(a, zL(a), z, U, V ).

Proof. We prove only (L) as the proof of (R) is symmetric. See Figure 23. Recall that b is
left of d, d is left of b′, and the pairs (a, b) and (a′, b′) are dangerous (by (I8)). Therefore,
by Proposition 45 (and transitivity – Proposition 37),
a /∈shad(b′), a /∈shad(d), a′ /∈shad(b), and a′ /∈shad(d).
In particular, d ∈Y (a), and so, we can fix z ∈Z(a) such that z ⩽d in P. Altogether, we
have
z′, b ∈Y (a′) and z, d ∈Y (a).
Since shad(z′) ⊆shad(b) (by Proposition 33), and a /∈shad(b) (by (I6)), we also obtain
a /∈shad(z′).
Claim 63. z′ is left of d, z′ is left of z, and z is left of b′.
Proof. First, we prove that z′ is left of d.
By assumption, a′ ∥d and a′ < z′ ⩽b in P.
We have already proved that a′ /∈shad(d) and z′, b ∈Y (a′). Finally, b is left of d, hence,
by Proposition 52.(L), z′ is left of d.
Next, we prove that z′ is left of z. Since a ∥b in P, we have a ∥z′ in P. Moreover, a < z ⩽d in
P. We have already proved that a /∈shad(z′), z, d ∈Y (a), and that z′ is left of d. Therefore,
by Proposition 52.(R), z′ is left of z.
Finally, we prove that z is left of b′. By assumption, a ∥b′ and a < z ⩽d in P. We have already
proved that a /∈shad(b′) and z, d ∈Y (a). Finally, d is left of b′, hence, by Proposition 52.(L),
z is left of b′. This completes the proof of the claim.
◁
Since b′ is left of zR(a′) (by Proposition 57), Claim 63 yields that z′ is left of z and z is left
of zR(a′). In particular, z′ is left of zR(a′), and so, we can define R = R(a′, z′, zR(a′), U, V ).
Moreover, by Corollary 61.(i), z ∈int R. Let W be an exposed witnessing path from a to z.
Suppose to the contrary that a /∈int R. Then, W intersects ∂R in an element not in B, say
u. It follows that a′ ⩽u < z ⩽d in P, which is a contradiction. This ends the proof.
□
6.3. Regular sequences. The goal of the next two subsections is to classify all alternating
cycles of size 2 with pairs in I. Eventually, we will split them into four types, see Corollary 70.
We say that a sequence ((a1, b1), . . . , (ak, bk)) is regular if (ai, bi) ∈I for every i ∈[k] and bi
is left of bi+1 for every i ∈[k −1]. Since the “left of” relation is transitive, see Proposition 37,
we note that in a regular sequence ((a1, b1), . . . , (ak, bk)), the elements b1, . . . , bk are linearly
ordered by the “left of” relation. Observe also that a subsequence of a regular sequence is
regular.
We list four key properties that a regular sequence ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) may or may not satisfy.
The first letter of the name of a property indicates if it concerns leftmost paths (L) or rightmost
paths (R). The ordering of the numbers 1 and 2 indicates the relative positions of a1 and a2
in the drawing, reading from left to right. See Figure 24. Properties labeled without a star
are simple, while those with a star are more involved (and in fact imply respective simpler
statements, see Observation 65).
(L12)
ML(a1) is left of ML(a2).
(L21⋆) There exist u ∈Z(a2) and an exposed witnessing path U from a2 to u in P such that
• u is an element of both WL(zL(a1)) and WL(b1),
• x0[WL(zL(a1))]u[U]a2 is left of WL(zL(a1)).
(R12)
MR(a2) is right of MR(a1).

If a regular sequence σ = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) satisfies (L21⋆), then for all u ∈Z(a2) and U as in
(L21⋆), we say that (u, U) witnesses (L21⋆) for σ. Sometimes, we omit U and just say that u
witnesses (L21⋆) for σ. Symmetrically, if σ satisfies (R21⋆), then for all v ∈Z(a1) and V as
in (R21⋆), we say that (v, V ) witnesses (R21⋆) for σ.
Note that for a regular sequence ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)), the satisfaction of each of the properties
does not depend on both b1 and b2, i.e. for each of the properties, one or both of these
elements can be replaced by other elements in B so that the sequence is still regular, and then
the property is still satisfied. More precisely, we have the following.

same applies to (R21⋆) and (R12). We highlight this in the next observation.
Observation 65. Let σ = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) be regular.
(L) If σ satisfies (L21⋆), then a2 < b1 in P and ML(a2) is left of ML(a1).
(R) If σ satisfies (R21⋆), then a1 < b2 in P and MR(a1) is right of MR(a2).
The following proposition shows that (L21⋆) (and (R21⋆)) implies a stronger prop-
erty.

Proposition 66. Let σ = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) be regular.
(L) If u ∈Z(a2) witnesses (L21⋆) for σ, then for every z ∈Z(a1) such that z is left of b1,
the element u lies in WL(z).
(R) If v ∈Z(a1) witnesses (R21⋆) for σ, then for every z ∈Z(a2) such that z is right of b2,
the element v lies in WR(z).
Proof. We prove only (L). The proof of (R) is symmetric. Assume that σ satisfies (L21⋆)
which is witnessed by u ∈Z(a2), and let z ∈Z(a1) such that z is left of b1. Recall that u lies
in both WL(zL(a1)) and WL(b1). We consider cases of how the paths WL(zL(a1)) and WL(z)
relate to each other.
If WL(zL(a1)) is a subpath of WL(z), then the assertion holds trivially as u lies in WL(zL(a1)).
If WL(z) is a subpath of WL(zL(a1)), then it suffices to argue that u < z in P.
Indeed,
otherwise a1 < z ⩽u ⩽b1 in P, which is a contradiction. It follows that u lies in WL(z).
Next assume that WL(zL(a1)) is left of WL(z). Since z is left of b1, in particular, WL(z) is
left of WL(b1). Again, recall that u lies in both WL(zL(a1)) and WL(b1). All this allows us to
apply Corollary 16 (the considered paths are pairwise x0-consistent by Proposition 15.(L)), to
obtain that u indeed lies in WL(z).
Finally, assume that WL(z) is left of WL(zL(a1)). Consider the path M = x0[WL(z)]z[W]a1,
where W is an exposed witnessing path from a1 to z in P.
Clearly, M ∈M(a1).
Since
x0[ML(a1)]zL(a1) = WL(zL(a1)) by Proposition 54, the path M is left of ML(a1), which
contradicts the definition of ML(a1), and shows that this case does not occur.
□
Next, we show that each of the four properties is transitive.
Proposition 67. Let ((a1, b1), (a2, b2), (a3, b3)) be regular. Let σ12 = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)), σ23 =
((a2, b2), (a3, b3)), and σ13 = ((a1, b1), (a3, b3)).
(i) If σ12 and σ23 satisfy (L12), then σ13 satisfies (L12).
(ii) If σ12 and σ23 satisfy (L21⋆), then σ13 satisfies (L21⋆).
(iii) If σ12 and σ23 satisfy (R12), then σ13 satisfies (R12).
(iv) If σ12 and σ23 satisfy (R21⋆), then σ13 satisfies (R21⋆).
Proof. The arguments for items (i) and (iii) are symmetric so we only include a proof of the
former. The same applies to items (ii) and (iv).
We start with an argument for (i). Suppose that σ12 and σ23 satisfy (L12). Therefore, ML(a1)
is left of ML(a2) and ML(a2) is left of ML(a3). By Proposition 9, this relation is transitive so
ML(a1) is left of ML(a3), as desired.
Now we proceed with the proof (ii). Suppose that σ12 and σ23 satisfy (L21⋆). Let u ∈Z(a2)
and U be an exposed witnessing path from a2 to u in P such that (u, U) witnesses (L21⋆) for
σ12 and let u′ ∈Z(a3) and U′ be an exposed witnessing path from a3 to u′ in P such that
(u′, U′) witnesses (L21⋆) for σ23. We claim that (u′, U′) witnesses (L21⋆) for σ13.
Since (a2, b2) ∈I, a2 < u ⩽b1 in P, and b1 left of b2, we can apply Corollary 53.(L) and
conclude that u is left of b2. Now, since σ23 is regular, u′ witnesses (L21⋆) for σ23, u ∈Z(a2),
and u left of b2, we can apply Proposition 66.(L) to σ23 and conclude that u′ lies in WL(u).
Since u witnesses (L21⋆) for σ12, u lies in both WL(zL(a1)) and WL(b1), and so, u′ lies in both
WL(zL(a1)) and WL(b1).

6.4. Classifying alternating cycles of size 2. Let ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) be an alternating cycle
in P with both pairs in I. Note that by (I7) either ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) or ((a2, b2), (a1, b1)) is
regular. The goal of this subsection is to classify regular alternating cycles of size 2 in P into
one of the four types. To this end, we associate some regions to each regular alternating cycle
of size 2. First, we need a simple property that is a corollary of Corollary 50.
Corollary 68. Let σ = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) be a regular alternating cycle in P. Then, b2 ∈Y (a1)
and b1 ∈Y (a2).
Let σ = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) be a regular alternating cycle in P. By Corollary 68, b2 ∈Y (a1) and
b1 ∈Y (a2). Thus, we can fix z1 ∈Z(a1) and z2 ∈Z(a2) such that z1 ⩽b2 and z2 ⩽b1 in P.
By Proposition 57, zL(a1) is left of b1 and by Corollary 53.(R), z1 is right of b1. Therefore,
by transitivity, zL(a1) is left of z1. Symmetrically, we obtain that z2 is left of zR(a2). Let W1
be an exposed witnessing path from a1 to z1 in P, and let W2 be an exposed witnessing path
from a2 to z2 in P. Let
R1 = R(a1, zL(a1), z1, a1[ML(a1)]zL(a1), W1),
R2 = R(a2, z2, zR(a2), W2, a2[MR(a2)]zR(a2)).
We say that R1 is a left region of σ and R2 is a right region of σ. Observe that by Corol-
lary 61.(i),
b1 ∈int R1 and b2 ∈int R2.
(7)
In the next proposition, we connect relative positions of a1, a2 and R1, R2 with the properties

Proposition 69. Let σ = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) be a regular alternating cycle. Let R1 be a left
region of σ and let R2 be a right region of σ.
(i) If a2 ∈int R1, then σ satisfies (L12).

(iii) If a1 ∈int R2, then σ satisfies (R12).
(iv) If a1 /∈R2, then σ satisfies (R21⋆).

a1 to z1 in P such that R1 = R(a1, zL(a1), z1, a1[ML(a1)]zL(a1), W1). Let q be the lower-min
of R1, and let m be the upper-min of R1. Let γL = x0[WL(zL(a1))]zL(a1)[ML(a1)]m and
γR = x0[WR(z1)]z1[W1]m.

Corollary 70. Let σ = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) be a regular alternating cycle in P with both pairs
in I.
σ satisfies (L12) ⇐⇒a2 ∈int R1 for every left region R1 of σ

⇐⇒a1 ∈int R2 for some right region R2 of σ.
(iii)
σ satisfies (R21⋆) ⇐⇒a1 ̸∈R2 for every right region R2 of σ
⇐⇒a1 ̸∈R2 for some right region R2 of σ.
(iv)
Therefore, all regular alternating cycles of size 2 can be classified into four types. We say that
a regular alternating cycle σ of size 2 in P with both pairs in I is:
In-In if σ satisfies (R12) and (L12);
In-Out if σ satisfies (R12) and (L21⋆);
Out-In if σ satisfies (R21⋆) and (L12);
Out-Out if σ satisfies (R21⋆) and (L21⋆).

7. Auxiliary oriented graphs: definitions and the coloring lemma
An orientation of a graph G is a function assigning to each edge {u, v} of G one of the pairs:
(u, v) or (v, u). An oriented graph is a graph with a fixed orientation. The vertex set of an
oriented graph H is the vertex set of the underlying graph, while the edge set of H is the
set of all (u, v) such that {u, v} is an edge in the underlying graph mapped to (u, v) by the
orientation. We say that an edge (u, v) of an oriented graph H is an edge from u to v in H.
A directed path is an oriented graph such that {v0, . . . , vk} is its vertex set and {(vi−1, vi) :
i ∈[k]} is its edge set, where v0, . . . , vk are pairwise distinct. This directed path starts in
v0 and ends in vk. The number of vertices of a directed path is its order. A directed cycle
is an oriented graph with at least three vertices such that removing each of its edges gives a
directed path. An oriented graph is acyclic if it contains no directed cycle.
When H is an acyclic oriented graph, we define max-path(H) as the maximum order of a
directed path in H. Moreover, for a vertex v of H, let max-start-path(H, v) be the maximum
order of a directed path in H starting in v.
In this section, we fix a maximal good instance (P, x0, G, e−∞, I). We study six auxiliary
oriented graphs – HOO, HIIL, HIIR, HIILR, HIO, and HOI – each with vertex set I and
edges given by carefully chosen regular alternating cycles of size 2. All six oriented graphs
are acyclic. Four of them – HOO, HIIL, HIIR, and HIILR – have the maximum order of a
directed path at most seP (I). The graphs HIO and HOI do not admit such a strong bound
in general, and instead, we present a much more subtle statement. We state each of these
results (Propositions 71 to 74 and Lemma 75) in this section, however, we postpone the proofs
to Section 8. These results give ground for the coloring κ of I in which κ((a, b)) for (a, b) ∈I
is defined according to the order of the longest directed path starting or ending in (a, b) in all
six oriented graphs. The number of colors used by κ on I will be in O(seP (I)8). Finally, the
key statement of this section, the Coloring Lemma, see Lemma 76, shows that pairs in I with
the same color under κ form a reversible set in P. This completes the proof of Theorem 49
up to the postponed proofs.
As mentioned above, I is the vertex set of each of the six auxiliary oriented graphs defined
in this section. Moreover, from the definitions, it follows that each edge ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) in
these graphs is a regular sequence. In particular, b1 is left of b2. Therefore, by the transitivity
of the “left of” relation (see Proposition 37), each of the six oriented graphs is acyclic.
Let HOO be the oriented graph with the vertex set I and σ = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) is an edge
in HOO if σ is a regular Out-Out alternating cycle in P.
Proposition 71. max-path(HOO) ⩽seP (I).
Let HII be the oriented graph with the vertex set I and σ = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) is an edge in HII
if σ is a regular In-In alternating cycle in P. The argument in the Coloring Lemma requires
us to deal with certain supergraphs of HII, namely HIIL and HIIR.
Let HIIL be the oriented graph with the vertex set I and σ = ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) is an edge
in HIIL if σ is an edge in HII or there exists a witness t ∈B for σ such that
(iil1) (a2, t) ∈I and ((a1, b1), (a2, t)) is an edge in HII,
(iil2) t is left of b2, and
(iil3) a1 ∥b2 in P.
Proposition 72. max-path(HIIL) ⩽seP (I).

Let H ∈{HIO, HOI}. We assign a weight of 0 or 1 to each edge of H. All cycle edges and
shifted edges in H are of weight 1 and all the remaining edges in H are of weight 0. The
weight of a path in H is the sum of the weights of its edges. Furthermore, for a vertex v of
H, max-start-weight(H, v) is the maximum weight of a directed path in H starting in v and
max-end-weight(H, v) is the maximum weight of a directed path in H ending in v.
Contrary to the four previously defined oriented graphs, the maximum order of a directed
path in HIO and HOI cannot be bounded in terms of seP (I). Also, the maximum weight
of a directed path cannot be bounded in terms of seP (I) (see Figure 45 later in the pa-
per). Within Lemma 75, we prove a weaker property that is good enough for our purposes
in Lemma 76.
Lemma 75. Let m = 2 seP (I) · (2 seP (I) + 6).
(i) If ((a, b), (a′, b′)) is an edge of weight 1 in HIO, then
max-start-weight(HIO, (a, b)) ̸≡max-start-weight(HIO, (a′, b′)) mod m.
(ii) If ((a, b), (a′, b′)) is an edge of weight 1 in HOI, then
max-end-weight(HOI, (a, b)) ̸≡max-end-weight(HOI, (a′, b′)) mod m.
We are ready to define the final coloring of I. Let
s = seP (I) and m = 2s · (2s + 6).

reversible.

ξ}. We show that Iξ is reversible. Suppose otherwise and let ((a1, b1), . . . , (ak, bk)) be a strict
alternating cycle in P with all the pairs in Iξ. The indices in [k] are considered cyclically, e.g.
ak+1 = a1.

8. Auxiliary oriented graphs: the proofs
The goal of this section is to prove all the statements given without proofs in Section 7
(Propositions 71 to 74 and Lemma 75). To this end, we again fix a maximal good instance
(P, x0, G, e−∞, I) throughout the section.
8.1. More on regions. In this subsection, we collect technical statements on regions that
are used later.
Proposition 86. Let (a, u, v, U, V, R, q, m, γL, γR) be a region tuple.
(L) zL(a) /∈int R, and moreover, if zL(a) ̸⩽u in P, then zL(a) /∈R.
(R) zR(a) /∈int R, and moreover, if zR(a) ̸⩽v in P, then zR(a) /∈R.
Proof. We prove only (L) as the proof of (R) is symmetric. Suppose to the contrary that
zL(a) ∈int R. Then, by Proposition 60.(ii), WL(zL(a)) is right of γL. It follows that WL(zL(a))
is right of M = x0[WL(u)]u[U]a.
Hence, by Proposition 54, ML(a) is right of M.
Since
M ∈M(a), this is a contradiction, and thus, we obtain zL(a) /∈int R.
Now, assume that zL(a) ̸⩽u in P, and suppose to the contrary that zL(a) ∈R. We already
proved that zL(a) /∈int R, hence, zL(a) ∈∂R. Moreover, since zL(a) ̸⩽u in P, the element
zL(a) does not lie in WL(u), which implies that zL(a) lies in WR(v). In particular, zL(a) ∈
shad(v). On the other hand, we claim that zL(a) /∈shad(u). Indeed, since u ∈Z(a), a /∈
shad(u), and so, by Proposition 51.(i), zL(a) does not lie in the interior of shad(u) (since
zL(a) ̸⩽u in P, zL(a) is not on the boundary of shad(u) either). Therefore, since u is left

Proposition 87. Let (a, u, v, U, V, R, q, m, γL, γR) be a region tuple. Let b ∈B with b ∈R.
Then, q lies in both WL(b) and WR(b).
Proof. We only prove that q lies in WL(b) as the proof that q lies in WR(b) is symmetric. Since
b ∈R and x0 /∈int R, the path WL(b) intersects ∂R, and so, it intersects u[WL(u)]q[WR(v)]v.
Let w be an element of WL(b) in ∂R. If w lies in u[WL(u)]q, then by Proposition 15.(L),
x0[WL(u)]w = x0[WL(b)]w, and so, q lies in WL(b) as desired. Thus, assume that w lies in
q[WR(v)]v and w ̸= q. Consider the path W = x0[WL(q)]q[WR(v)]w[WL(b)]b. If WL(b) = W,
then the assertion holds, thus, assume that WL(b) ̸= W. It follows that WL(b) is left of W.
Next, we study the relation between WL(q) and WL(b). Since q ⩽b in P, it is not possible
that WL(b) is a strict subpath of WL(q). If WL(q) is a subpath of WL(b), then q lies in WL(b),
as desired. If WL(q) is left of WL(b), then W is left of WL(b), which is false. Finally, if WL(b)
is left of WL(q), the path x0[WL(b)]w[WR(v)]v is left of WL(u) = x0[WL(q)]q[WL(u)]u, which
contradicts the fact that u is left of v. This ends the proof.
□
Corollary 88. Let (a, u, v, U, V, R, q, m, γL, γR) be a region tuple.

If u′, v′ ∈R, then,
u′[WL(u′)]q′[WR(v′)]v′ ⊆R.

particular, q ⩽q′ in P and by Proposition 59, both paths q[WL(u′)]u′ and q[WR(v′)]v′ are
contained in R. This completes the proof.
□

Proposition 91. Let (a, u, v, U, V, R, q, m, γL, γR) be a region tuple. Let d ∈B with d /∈R.
(L) Let u be left of d and let w be the maximal common element of WL(d) and WR(v) in P.
For every b ∈B with b ∈R and b ∈shad(d), we have b ∈shad(w). Moreover, if such
b ∈B exists, then q ⩽w in P.
(R) Let v be right of d and w be the maximal common element of WR(d) and WL(u) in P.
For every b ∈B with b ∈R and b ∈shad(d), we have b ∈shad(w). Moreover, if such
b ∈B exists, then q ⩽w in P.
Proof. We prove only (L) as the proof of (R) is symmetric. See again Figure 33. Let b ∈B
with b ∈R and b ∈shad(d).
First, we show the “moreover” part, that is, q ⩽w in P. Since u is left of d, we have WL(u)
left of WL(d). Moreover, WL(q) is a subpath of WL(u), hence, either WL(q) is left of WL(d)
or WL(q) is a subpath of WL(d). In the latter case, q lies in both WL(d) and WL(w), hence,
q ⩽w in P, as desired. Thus, we may assume that WL(q) is left of WL(d). Since b ∈shad(d),
by Proposition 30.(L), WL(b) is not left of WL(d). This translates into one of the three options:
WL(d) is a subpath of WL(b) or WL(b) is a subpath of WL(d) or WL(d) is left of WL(b). Since
b ∈R, by Proposition 87, q lies in WL(b), and so, WL(q) is a subpath of WL(b). Each of the
options “WL(d) is a subpath of WL(b)” and “WL(d) is left of WL(b)” implies that WL(q) is
left of WL(b), which is false. Thus, the third option holds: WL(b) is a subpath of WL(d). It
follows that WL(q) is a subpath of WL(d), and so, q ⩽w in P, as desired.
Now, we prove the main statement. To this end, suppose to the contrary that b /∈shad(w).
By Proposition 90.(L).(i), either w < b in P or WL(b) is left of WL(w). In the latter case,
we obtain WL(b) left of WL(d), which contradicts b ∈shad(d) by Proposition 30.(L). Thus,
assume that w < b in P and let W be a witnessing path from w to b in P.
Note that w ̸= d as w ∈R and d /∈R. Let e−and e+ be the edges immediately preceding
and following w in WL(d) (when w = x0, we set e−= e−∞). Let f−and f+ be the edges
immediately preceding and following w in γR (when w = x0, we set f−= e−∞). Let e be the
edge of W incident to w. See Figure 35.
Note that
f−≺f+ ≺e+ in the w-ordering
by Proposition 89.(L),
(12)
f−≺e ≼f+ in the w-ordering
by (rg3) or (rg1).
(13)
Next, we claim that
f−≼e−≺f+ in the w-ordering.
(14)
First, assume that q < w in P. Then, since q, w ∈R, by Proposition 59, q[WL(w)]w ⊆R,
and so, e−⊆R. Thus, by (rg4), (14) follows. Finally, assume that q = w. Then, f+ does not
lie in shad(q), and so, by (sb2), (14) follows.
By (13) and (14), either f−≺e ≺e−in the w-ordering or e−≺e ≼f+ in the w-ordering.
The former implies (by (sb2)) that e lies in shad(w), which yields b ∈shad(w), which is
a contradiction.
Therefore, the latter holds.
Combining it with (12), (13), and (14), we
obtain e−≺e ≼f+ ≺e+ in the w-ordering. This implies that x0[WL(w)]w[W]b is left of
WL(d), and thus, WL(b) is left of WL(d). However, this is a contradiction with b ∈shad(d)
by Proposition 30.(L). This completes the proof.
□

Proof of Proposition 71. If max-path(HOO) ⩽1, then there is nothing to prove as seP (I) ⩾1.
Thus, we can assume max-path(HOO) ⩾2. Let ((a1, b1), . . . , (an, bn)) be a path in HOO where
n is an integer with n ⩾2. By Proposition 67, ((ai, bi), (aj, bj)) satisfies (L21⋆) and (R21⋆)
for every i, j ∈[n] with i < j. Therefore, by Observation 65, aj < bi and ai < bj in P. It
follows that J = {(ai, bi) : i ∈[n]} induces a standard example in P. Since J ⊆I and |J| = n,
we obtain n ⩽seP (I), as desired.
□

Now, we prove the transitivity of regular In-In alternating cycles, which will give Proposi-
tion 94, that is, max-path(HII) ⩽seP (I). See examples of paths in HII in Figure 37.
Proposition 93. Let ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) and ((a2, b2), (a3, b3)) be regular In-In alternating cy-
cles. Then, ((a1, b1), (a3, b3)) is a regular In-In alternating cycle.
Proof. Let σ = ((a1, b1), (a3, b3)). Since b1 is left of b2 and b2 is left of b3, b1 is left of b3
(by Proposition 37), hence, σ is regular. By Proposition 67.(i) and (iii), σ satisfies (L12)
and (R12). Therefore, it suffices to prove that σ is an alternating cycle, that is, a1 < b3 and
a3 < b1 in P. We only prove that a1 < b3 in P, the proof of the other inequality is symmetric.
By Corollary 68, b1, b3 ∈Y (a2), thus, we can fix z1, z3 ∈Z(a2) such that z1 ⩽b1 and z3 ⩽b3
in P. Let W1 and W3 be exposed witnessing paths in P from a2 to z1 and z3 respectively.
Since b1 is left of b2 and b2 is left of b3, by Corollary 53, z1 is left of b2 and b2 is left of
z3. In particular, z1 is left of z3, and so, we can define the region S = R(a2, z1, z3, W1, W3).
We consider two cases: either a1 ∈S or a1 /∈S. Note that neither of the cases leads to a
contradiction, see Figure 38.
First, assume that a1 ∈S. By Proposition 92, R(a1) = R(a2) = R(a3), and so, zL(a1) =
zL(a2) = zL(a3) =: z. Note that a1, a2, a3 ⩽z in P, hence, z ̸⩽b1, b3 in P, and thus, z ̸⩽z1, z3
in P. Therefore, by Proposition 86, z /∈S. Let W be an exposed witnessing path from a1 to
z in P. Since a1 ∈S and z /∈S, there is an element w of W in ∂S. Since W is an exposed
path and a1 ∥b1 in P, w lies in W3. It follows that a1 < w < z3 ⩽b3 in P, as desired.
Finally, assume that a1 /∈S. Since z1 is left of b2 and b2 is left of z3, by Corollary 61.(i), we
have b2 ∈int S. Let W be a witnessing path from a1 to b2 in P. Since a1 /∈S and b2 ∈S,
there is an element w of W in ∂S. Since a1 ∥b1 and a2 ∥b2 in P, w lies in WR(z3). It follows
that a1 < w ⩽z3 ⩽b3 in P.

Corollary 100.
(L) For all positive integers n and for each path ((a1, b1), . . . , (an, bn)) in HIIL, there exist
t2, . . . , tn ∈B such that ((a1, b1), (a2, t2), . . . , (an, tn)) is a path in HII.
(R) For all positive integers n and for each path ((a1, b1), . . . , (an, bn)) in HIIR, there exist
s1, . . . , sn−1 ∈B such that ((a1, s1), . . . , (an−1, sn−1), (an, bn)) is a path in HII.
Proof. We only prove (L) as the proof of (R) is symmetric.
We proceed by induction
on n.
When n = 1, the statement is vacuously satisfied.
Suppose that n ⩾2 and
let ((a1, b1), . . . , (an, bn)) be a path in HIIL.
By induction, there exist t3, . . . tn such that
((a2, b2), (a3, t3), . . . , (an, tn)) is a path in HII. If ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) is a cycle edge in HIIL, then
it suffices to set t2 = b2. Thus, assume that ((a1, b1), (a2, b2)) is a shifted edge in HIIL, say wit-
nessed by t ∈B. We claim that the assertion is satisfied for t2 = t. By (iil1), ((a1, b1), (a2, t)) is
an edge in HII. Hence, when n = 2, the statement holds. When n ⩾3, by Proposition 95.(L),
((a2, t), (a3, t3)) is an edge in HII. This ends the proof.
□
Proof of Propositions 72 and 73. Corollary 100 implies that
max-path(HIIL) ⩽max-path(HII) and
max-path(HIIR) ⩽max-path(HII).
Proposition 94 states that max-path(HII) ⩽seP (I), and so, we obtain
max-path(HIIL) ⩽seP (I) and
max-path(HIIR) ⩽seP (I).
□

Claim 105. For all i, j ∈[n −1] with i < j,
(i) uj < ui in P,
(ii) WL(uj) is a proper subpath of WL(ui),
(iii) ai ∥uj in P.
Proof. Let i, j ∈[n −1] with i < j. By (u5) and (u8), uj ⩽π(bj−1) < uj−1 in P, thus, (i)
follows from a simple induction. Item (ii) follows from the fact that both ui and uj lie in W ⋆
(by (u3)) and (i). For the proof of (iii) suppose to the contrary that ai < uj or uj ⩽ai in P.
Note that the latter can not hold as ai /∈B and uj ∈B. Thus, we assume ai < uj in P.
Then, ai < uj ⩽ui+1 ⩽π(bi) ⩽bi in P, where the second inequality follows from (i) and the
third inequality follows from (u8). Clearly, ai < bi in P is a contradiction, which completes
the proof.

Claim 110. For all i, j ∈E with i < j,
(i) vj ̸⩽vi in P;
(ii) if σi is a shifted edge, then vj /∈Ri;
(iii) if σi is a cycle edge, then vj /∈∂Ri;
(iv) if σi is a cycle edge and vj ∈int Ri, then vi lies in WR(vj);
(v) if σi is a cycle edge and vj ∈int Ri, then vi lies in WR(bi+1).
Proof. See Figure 49 for an illustration. Let i, j ∈E with i < j. Suppose to the contrary that
vj ⩽vi in P. Let di+1 = bi+1 when σi is a cycle edge and di+1 = ti+1 when σi is a shifted
edge. If i + 1 = j then ai+1 < vi+1 ⩽vi ⩽di+1 in P, which is a clear contradiction. Thus,
we assume i + 1 < j. Now we have that di+1 is left of bj (by (io2) when di+1 = ti+1). Since
vj ⩽vi in P, we have, vj ∈shad(vi) ⊆shad(di+1) (by Proposition 33). By Claim 107.(i), bj is
left of vj, thus, vj /∈shad(bj). Therefore, we can apply Proposition 36.(L), to obtain that vj
is left of bj, which is again a contradiction that completes the proof of (i).
For the proof of (ii) observe that if σi is a shifted edge, then vi is left of bi+1 (by Claim 107.(iii)),
bi+1 is either left of bj or equal to bj, and bj is left of vj (by Claim 107.(i)), hence, vi is left of
vj. Therefore, by Corollary 61.(ii), vj /∈Ri.
From now on, assume that σi is a cycle edge.
All elements of ∂Ri that are in B are in
WL(ui) ∪WR(vi). Since ui ∥vj in P (by Claim 108.(iii)), vj does not lie in WL(ui). By (i), vj
does not lie in WR(vi). This completes the proof of (iii).
From now on, assume also that vj ∈int Ri. Let w = gcpe(WR(vj), γR,i). In particular, w lies
in WR(vi). If w = vi, then the assertion holds. Thus, suppose to the contrary that w < vi
in P. Since vj ∈int Ri, by Proposition 60.(ii), WR(vj) is left of γR,i, and so, of WR(vi). Let
W be a witnessing path from vi to bi+1 in P. Note that x0[WR(vi)]vi[W]bi+1 is either left of
or equal to WR(bi+1). It follows that WR(vj) is left of WR(bi+1). However, bi+1 is left of vj
by Claim 108.(ii), thus, we obtained a contradiction. This proves (iv).
The path x0[WR(vi)]vi[W]bi+1 is either left of or equal to WR(bi+1). Observe that either vi
lies in WR(bi+1) or WR(vi) is left of WR(bi+1). Thus, suppose the latter. Recall that bi+1
is left of vj, and in particular, WR(bi+1) is left of WR(vj). We obtain that WR(vi) is left of
WR(vj), which is false by (iv). This completes the proof of (v).

and so, WL(uj) is left of WL(vj). This completes the proof of (i) in the case, where vi,j = vj.
Next, assume that vi,j = zR(ai+1). Since ai+1 ∥vi in P (by Claim 107.(iv)), zR(ai+1) does

9. Proof of the main result
In this section, we wrap up the proof of the main theorem. In fact, we prove a more gen-
eral statement (Theorem 131), which immediately implies that the class of posets that are
subposets of posets with planar cover graphs is also dim-bounded (Corollary 132). Note that
a subposet of a poset with a planar cover graph does not necessarily have a planar cover
graph, e.g. standard examples. Finally, note that Theorem 131 implies Theorem 1 by taking
I = Inc(P).
Theorem 131. For every poset P with a planar cover graph and for every I ⊆Inc(P),
dimP (I) ⩽64s6 · (s + 3)2 + 12,
where s = seP (Inc(P) ∩(π1(I) × π2(I)).
Proof. Let P be a poset with a planar cover graph and let I ⊆Inc(P). By Corollary 13, there
exists an instance (P ′, x′
0, G′, e′
−∞, I′) such that P ′ −x′
0 is a convex subposet of P and I′ ⊆I,
or P ′ −x′
0 is a convex subposet of P −1 and I′ ⊆I−1, and
dimP (I) ⩽2 dimP ′(I′).
In particular, since P ′ −x′
0 is a subposet of P and I′ ⊆I or P ′ −x′
0 is a subposet of P −1 and
I′ ⊆I−1 we have
seP ′(Inc(P ′) ∩(π1(I′) × π2(I′)) ⩽seP (Inc(P) ∩(π1(I) × π2(I)).
By Corollary 47, there exists a good instance (Q, x0, G, e−∞, J) such that Q is a convex
subposet of P ′, J ⊆I′, and
dimP ′(I′) ⩽2 dimQ(J) + 6.
In particular, since Q is a subposet of P ′ and J ⊆I′
seQ(Inc(Q) ∩(π1(J) × π2(J)) ⩽seP ′(Inc(P ′) ∩(π1(I′) × π2(I′)).
By Proposition 48,
there exists J

Inc(Q) ∩(π1(J) × π2(J)) such that
(Q, x0, G, e−∞, J+) is a maximal good instance. In particular,
dimQ(J) ⩽dimQ(J+) and
seQ(J+) ⩽seQ(Inc(Q) ∩(π1(J) × π2(J)).
By Theorem 49,
dimQ(J+) ⩽16 seQ(J+)6 · (seQ(J+) + 3)2.

Observe that
seQ(J+) ⩽seQ(Inc(Q) ∩(π1(J) × π2(J))
⩽seP ′(Inc(P ′) ∩(π1(I′) × π2(I′))
⩽seP (Inc(P) ∩(π1(I) × π2(I)).
Summarizing, we obtain
dimP (I) ⩽2 dimP ′(I′)
⩽4 dimQ(J) + 12
⩽4 dimQ(J+) + 12
⩽4(16 seQ(J+)6 · (seQ(J+) + 3)2) + 12
⩽64s6 · (s + 3)2 + 12,
where s = se(Inc(P) ∩(π1(I) × π2(I)).
□
Corollary 132. The class of posets that are subposets of posets with planar cover graphs is
dim-bounded.
Proof. Let f(s) = 64s6·(s+3)2+12 for every positive integer s. Let P be a poset with a planar
cover graph and let Q be a subposet of P. Let I ⊆Inc(P) be the set of all incomparable pairs
(a, b) in P such that both a and b are elements of Q. By definition, I = Inc(P) ∩(π1(I) ×
π2(I)). In particular, I = Inc(Q), dimQ(I) = dimP (I), and seQ(I) = seP (I). Therefore,
by Theorem 131,
dim(Q) = dimQ(I) = dimP (I) ⩽f(seP (I)) = f(seQ(I)) = f(se(Q)).
□
